3 ML Lifecycle

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why is seeing the whole map necessary before walking any single path?

Engineering organizations often split an ML system into three specialties, with one team curating datasets, another tuning model architectures, and a third provisioning serving hardware. The components those teams own remain coupled. The volume and quality of collected data bound which algorithms are viable, the computational structure of the chosen algorithm dictates what hardware can execute it efficiently, and the memory and bandwidth of the target hardware reshape what preprocessing can run at inference. Optimizing each in isolation tends to produce accurate models that cannot be deployed and efficient data pipelines that serve the wrong distributions, and the cost of such a mismatch compounds with how late it surfaces. A memory limit discovered during initial design changes one line of a hardware specification; the same limit discovered on the eve of deployment discards months of training runs, annotation budgets, and integration work. Before analyzing any single component, an engineer needs the full map of how an ML system is specified, trained, validated, deployed, and sustained as one operational loop. In D·A·M terms, the ML lifecycle is where data, algorithm, and machine are co-designed over time, each stage passing constraints to the others until their combined capability meets real-world requirements.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the six ML lifecycle stages as coordinated data-algorithm-machine decisions with feedback
  • Compare ML workflows with traditional software using drift, nondeterminism, and operational feedback
  • Construct a problem definition that states a request as a measurable improvement over an existing baseline
  • Analyze how problem-definition constraints propagate through data, modeling, validation, and deployment
  • Estimate rework from a late-discovered constraint by tracing dependent data, model, validation, and deployment artifacts
  • Evaluate accuracy, efficiency, reproducibility, and deployment readiness trade-offs across lifecycle stages
  • Design feedback loops that connect monitoring signals to retraining, maintenance, and revised requirements
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

3.1 The Lifecycle as a Loop

Building an ML system takes far more than training a model. A team must decide what the system has to do and within which limits, gather and prepare the data it learns from, train and test the model, install it where it will run, and keep checking that it still works as the world around it changes. System Paradigms examined one of those limits in depth, showing that the deployment target can change the engineering of the same model by orders of magnitude between a phone and a data center. The rest of this book takes the remaining work apart piece by piece along the data, algorithm, and machine axes, from data pipelines and model architectures through training, optimization, and serving. Those pieces are not independent. Each hands its results to the next, and a mistake made in one tends to surface in another. Understanding any single piece therefore requires first understanding how they connect: the sequence of stages every ML system moves through, what each stage hands to the next, and how a constraint discovered in one place reaches the others.

That sequence is the machine learning lifecycle, a structured, iterative process1 that guides the development, evaluation, and improvement of ML systems (Amershi et al. 2019). Numbered in the order a project first meets them, its six stages form a chain in which each stage produces the artifact the next one needs. Problem definition (1) turns a request into measurable targets and deployment constraints. These guide data collection and preparation (2), which turns raw observations into a versioned, validated dataset. Model development and training (3) then fits trained weights to that dataset, and evaluation and validation (4) tests those weights against the original targets and records the release evidence in a release manifest. Once the manifest clears the gate, deployment and integration (5) installs the approved model where it must run, while monitoring and maintenance (6) watches the live system and sends what it learns back to earlier stages, closing the loop drawn in figure 1.

1 CRISP-DM (cross-industry standard process for data mining): CRISP-DM codified data-intensive system development as six interconnected, iterative phases rather than a linear waterfall (Chapman et al. 2000); its core design principle (moving back and forth between phases as results require) directly informs the modern ML lifecycle’s structure.

Chapman, Pete, Julian Clinton, Randy Kerber, Thomas Khabaza, Thomas Reinartz, Colin Shearer, and Rudiger Wirth. 2000. “CRISP-DM 1.0: Step-by-Step Data Mining Guide.” SPSS Inc 1: 78.
Amershi, Saleema, Andrew Begel, Christian Bird, Robert DeLine, Harald Gall, Ece Kamar, Nachiappan Nagappan, Besmira Nushi, and Thomas Zimmermann. 2019. “Software Engineering for Machine Learning: A Case Study.” 2019 IEEE/ACM 41st International Conference on Software Engineering: Software Engineering in Practice (ICSE-SEIP), 291–300. https://doi.org/10.1109/icse-seip.2019.00042.

The return arrow from monitoring is what makes the lifecycle a loop rather than a checklist. Evidence flows back from later stages to earlier ones, and constraints should flow back with it. A memory limit set by the deployment device belongs in problem definition, where it can shape the dataset, the model, and the evaluation, not in deployment, where it can only reject them.

Definition 3.1: Machine learning lifecycle

Machine learning lifecycle is the iterative engineering process of building, deploying, monitoring, and updating ML systems, where evidence from later stages can feed back to earlier stages because model performance may change after deployment.

  1. Significance: The lifecycle is a closed loop, not a linear pipeline. Each stage hands the next an artifact it depends on (targets, a dataset, trained weights, release evidence, a running service, monitoring evidence). A decision at one stage therefore constrains every stage after it, and evidence from a later stage can force an earlier one to be revisited. How often the loop must turn is itself a recurring budget, set by how quickly the deployment environment changes and how long verified labels take to arrive.
  2. Distinction: Unlike traditional software, whose behavior changes when code, configuration, dependencies, or environment changes, ML behavior can also change as the world changes. A deployed model’s accuracy may change through distribution shift even when the code, infrastructure, and configuration remain untouched.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating deployment as the end of the lifecycle. Retraining decisions depend on outcome evidence that exists only after deployment.

Carrying constraints around that loop is the job of the ML workflow, the engineering framework that makes constraints explicit at each stage and traces how deployment constraints propagate across Data, Algorithm, and Machine. The two terms divide the job. The lifecycle is what gets traversed, the stages themselves; the workflow is how the traversal is managed, the engineering discipline of orchestrating them. The workflow marks the shift from model researcher to systems engineer. A researcher optimizes individual elements: a better architecture, a cleaner dataset, a faster accelerator. A systems engineer orchestrates those elements into production systems that reliably deliver value.

↩︎ The deployment paradigms whose memory, latency, and power constraints the workflow propagates are established in Four Deployment Regimes Across Nine Orders of Magnitude.

In practice, the stages between problem definition and deployment run as two pipelines on different clocks. Figure 2 zooms into that work as ten finer-grained blocks. The upper data pipeline, the substance of stage 2, continuously transforms raw operational streams through ingestion, curation, annotation, and validation into versioned, ML-ready datasets. The lower model pipeline executes the inner development loop of stages 3 and 4: training architectures, computing evaluation metrics, validating system constraints, and packaging approved artifacts for deployment, with stage 4’s release manifest drawn as an ML certificate. Outer-loop feedback arrows carry production telemetry and validation discoveries back to upstream data stages, the return path that stage 6 operates.

↪ The operational tooling and infrastructure that automate these lifecycle stages at scale are developed in ML Operations.

3.1.1 ML vs. traditional software

The loop and its two pipelines exist because of one difference between ML systems and traditional software. Both iterate.2 The difference that forces new workflow controls is where behavior comes from. Rule-based software changes behavior when someone changes the code, configuration, or dependencies. An ML system learns its behavior from data, so that behavior can also change when the data changes while the code stays fixed, and only fresh labeled outcomes can show whether today’s model still meets today’s requirement. Every workflow addition this chapter introduces (versioned datasets,3 statistical evaluation gates, drift monitoring) exists to manage that second path.

2 Waterfall model: A plan-driven lifecycle is often depicted as a sequence of requirements, implementation, and testing, but Royce (1970) warned that a purely sequential implementation was risky and described iteration between successive phases. ML systems add data and model feedback that must be managed explicitly.

Royce, Winston W. 1970. “Managing the Development of Large Software Systems.” Proceedings of IEEE WESCON 26: 328–88.

3 Data versioning: Unlike code, which changes through discrete, auditable commits, data can drift gradually (distribution shift), suddenly (schema migration), or subtly (label quality degradation). Plain Git is impractical for many multi-terabyte datasets, so teams commonly version external-data manifests or pointers using tools such as DVC and Git LFS. Without data versioning, teams cannot reliably reproduce a prior training run or determine whether an accuracy regression stems from a code change or a data change.

↩︎ Data-defined behavior, and the silent degradation it makes possible, are established in When ML Systems Fail Silently.

That difference alters how lifecycle stages interact. While conventional software engineering relies on production feedback to refine code and requirements, ML systems introduce feedback loops where operational data reshapes training distributions, detected drift prompts investigation, and runtime errors expose dataset blind spots. Table 1 contrasts traditional software and ML engineering across six core lifecycle dimensions, highlighting how data evolution redefines testing, deployment, and ongoing maintenance.

Checkpoint 3.1: ML vs. traditional software

ML systems are not traditional software with a model attached. Check the differences that force a separate workflow:

Each difference in the comparison traces back to behavior learned from data, which ties every stage to the stages around it. A diagram shows where the stages sit, but seeing how tightly data-learned behavior binds them requires tracing one production system across the full loop.

3.1.2 The running case: Diabetic retinopathy screening

Automated screening for diabetic retinopathy (DR), a major cause of preventable vision loss,4 exposes that stage-to-stage coupling in practice. While early detection prevents severe vision loss, limited access to specialist ophthalmologists in rural regions motivates automated screening programs. Google built one of the best documented of these programs, a deep-learning model that performed at the level of ophthalmologists on curated retinal images (Gulshan et al. 2016) and that Google Health then deployed in clinics in Thailand (Beede et al. 2020). Detecting the pathology, however, requires identifying subtle microvascular features under variable capture conditions. Figure 3 contrasts a normal retina (Panel A) with one showing diabetic retinopathy (Panel B).

4 Diabetic retinopathy (DR): DR is a common complication of diabetes; early detection can prevent vision loss, but specialist access varies substantially across countries. This gap motivates scalable screening programs. In clinics with limited hardware or unreliable connectivity, model size, offline capability, latency, and workflow integration can become binding deployment constraints.

Google’s diabetic retinopathy screening program spans both sides of the lab-to-field divide, making it a revealing system to trace across the full lifecycle. Its controlled validation documented expert-level accuracy, while its deployment in Thai clinics exposed operational friction the validation never saw: images rejected as ungradable, retakes that added work for nurses, and screening delayed when slow clinic internet connections held up uploads to the remote grading service.5

5 Healthcare AI deployment gap: In the Thai DR deployment studied by Beede et al. (2020), 393 of 1,838 submitted images (21 percent) failed the system’s image-quality requirements, often because clinic lighting, camera maintenance, or dilation practices did not match its assumptions. The case shows why laboratory metrics alone do not establish deployment readiness.

Beede, Emma, Elizabeth Baylor, Fred Hersch, Anna Iurchenko, Lauren Wilcox, Paisan Ruamviboonsuk, and Laura M. Vardoulakis. 2020. “A Human-Centered Evaluation of a Deep Learning System Deployed in Clinics for the Detection of Diabetic Retinopathy.” Proceedings of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI), 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/3313831.3376718.

To put concrete numbers on each stage, our running scenario adapts that case study into a composite clinic architecture. Because the remote grading path depended on each clinic’s internet connection, the scenario moves inference into the clinic. It describes a network of rural clinics, each executing local inference on an Orin Nano-class edge device with 4 to 8 GB of shared memory and an intermittent wide-area uplink of roughly 2 Mbps. The clinical mandate requires more than 90 percent sensitivity and a result the nurse can act on before the patient leaves, which means within seconds of capture and even when the uplink is down. Sensitivity measures the fraction of true disease cases correctly flagged. Its denominator includes only patients with the condition, so high aggregate accuracy on an imbalanced, mostly healthy population provides no assurance that sick patients will be detected. Specificity measures the fraction of healthy patients correctly left unflagged, bounding unnecessary referrals that would overwhelm regional specialists.

A team that runs these stages as a line rather than a loop fails in a predictable way. Day one: “Build a diabetic retinopathy screening model for rural clinics.” Day 90: 92 percent sensitivity on the validation set used to guide development. Day 120: 94 percent sensitivity after a month of architecture tuning. Day 150: the model is handed to deployment engineers. Day 151: deployment engineers report that the model needs 4 GB of memory to run. Day 152: someone checks the deployment target, a low-power edge device in each clinic whose 4–8 GB of memory is shared with the operating system, the camera pipeline, and the clinic software; the clinic’s IT plan reserves about 500 MB of it for the model. Day 153: five months of work is discarded.

The model’s accuracy was excellent. The team’s machine learning skills were excellent. The failure was a workflow failure. A deployment constraint that should have shaped every decision from day one was discovered only after the work was done. That 500 MB budget should have propagated backward to the first architecture meeting, constraining which models were even worth considering. Instead, the team optimized each component in isolation (data collection, architecture selection, training), and the integration failure appeared only when the pieces were assembled. The day-153 failure is one instance of a general principle about how constraints travel through the lifecycle.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Team A ships a diabetic retinopathy (DR) screening model and freezes all development once the model clears validation in the lab, treating subsequent tasks as standard server operations. Team B treats the launch as the beginning of an ongoing feedback loop, monitoring operational telemetry and data distributions to guide investigation and evidence-based model updates. Which team’s posture aligns with the ML lifecycle as defined in this chapter, and why?

    1. A. Team B, because the ML lifecycle is a closed loop where operational feedback, distribution drift, and real-world performance continuously reshape upstream data and model decisions.
    2. B. Team A, because once a model meets its offline validation thresholds, its statistical properties remain fixed and require only standard infrastructure maintenance.
    3. C. Team A, because changing a validated model in production introduces regulatory risk that outweighs the benefits of adapting to data drift.
    4. D. Team B, because the ML lifecycle mandates automatic daily retraining of production models regardless of whether input distributions have drifted.

  2. In the running case’s day-153 failure scenario, a team spends five months developing a diagnostic model that reaches 94 percent sensitivity, only to have the entire project discarded on day 153. Explain the root cause of this failure from a workflow perspective and state the systems engineering rule that would have prevented it.

  3. True or False: Traditional software workflows and ML lifecycles differ fundamentally because ML system behavior can degrade through data distribution drift over time even when application source code, execution environment, and hardware configuration remain completely untouched.

See Answers →

3.2 How Decisions Cascade: The Constraint Propagation Principle

In the DR scenario, the in-visit requirement already ruled out cloud inference and so required edge deployment. The edge target’s memory and power budgets then constrain model architecture, favoring compact MobileNet-class models over large ensembles (several models whose predictions are combined) and requiring automated image-quality checks at capture time. Each downstream constraint narrows the design space for upstream stages, coupling hardware limits directly to data collection and model design.

Definition 3.2: Constraint propagation principle

The constraint propagation principle states that a constraint discovered late in the ML lifecycle can force rework across affected upstream stages. Datasets, trained weights, validation evidence, and deployment packages record decisions that later work depends on. Correcting a missing constraint requires tracing those dependencies to determine which artifacts must change and which remain valid. Estimating actual rework requires the effort to revise each affected artifact, repeat its dependent work, and validate the resulting system.

  1. Significance: A latency target discovered at deployment (stage 5), such as 100 ms in a web service, may propagate backward. It can constrain model size (stage 3: operation count \(\Operations\)), dataset requirements (stage 2: dataset size \(\DatasetSize\), the number of training examples), and problem definition (stage 1: what accuracy is achievable). Rework depends on which earlier decisions relied on the missing constraint. Within the iron law, a latency budget bounds total time \(\Time\), and therefore every term that sums to it. It thus redefines the feasible region for \(\Operations\), for the bytes moved \(\DataVolume\) (distinct from the example count \(\DatasetSize\)), and for the hardware efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw\) the model must achieve.
  2. Distinction: Unlike modular decomposition (which encourages independent optimization of each component), this principle mandates end-to-end reasoning, because an accuracy optimum reached in isolation can be an infeasible system.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating deployment as “the last step” and so deferring its latency, memory, and power limits until they can only reject finished work.

Propagation runs in both directions, so the stages form a network of constraints rather than a chain. Backward propagation carries a deployment limit upstream. When rural clinic deployment reveals tight bandwidth, the team can compress images before sending them over the uplink. Lossy compression degrades input quality, however, so the training dataset must then include matching compression artifacts, or the model will see different inputs in service than in training (training-serving skew). Shifting inference entirely to the edge and transmitting only the diagnostic result relieves the uplink instead, but tightens the edge device’s memory capacity and power budget. Forward propagation carries an early data decision downstream. Capturing fundus images at the resolution needed to see small hemorrhages fixes the model’s input size, and with it the operation count every inference must pay on the clinic device. Upstream data choices and downstream machine boundaries remain physically coupled.

Decisions made without accounting for downstream execution boundaries accumulate technical debt.6 The stage interface specification (table 2) puts this principle to work by making constraints explicit at each stage boundary, so the team catches violations before handing an artifact downstream. When propagation occurs specifically through data quality failures, the resulting pattern is known as a data cascade, a chain of downstream failures triggered by bad data (Sambasivan et al. 2021) that Data cascades examines. Propagation explains why a late constraint is expensive; how much time each loop costs, and where that time goes, is the next question.

6 ML technical debt: Sculley et al. (2015) identify ML-specific debt mechanisms such as entanglement (changing one feature affects all others because the model learned joint distributions), hidden feedback loops (predictions influence future training data), and undeclared consumers (downstream systems depending on outputs without contracts). Since ML code is often only a small part of a production system, the surrounding configuration, pipelines, and infrastructure can allow this debt to accumulate silently.

Sculley, D., Gary Holt, Daniel Golovin, Eugene Davydov, Todd Phillips, Dietmar Ebner, Vinay Chaudhary, Michael Young, Jean-François Crespo, and Dan Dennison. 2015. “Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 2503–11.
Sambasivan, Nithya, Shivani Kapania, Hannah Highfill, Diana Akrong, Praveen Paritosh, and Lora M Aroyo. 2021. “‘Everyone Wants to Do the Model Work, Not the Data Work’: Data Cascades in High-Stakes AI.” Proceedings of the 2021 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems, 1–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445518.

↪ Shared contracts and deployment paths that reject incompatible changes before they reach production are enforced in Development Infrastructure.

  1. Which interpretation of the Constraint Propagation Principle is consistent with the chapter’s treatment of discovering constraints late in the ML lifecycle?

    1. A. It holds that a late-discovered constraint can force rework across upstream stages, with the actual rework depending on which datasets, weights, validation evidence, and deployment packages relied on the missing constraint.
    2. B. It establishes a universal linear rule: each later lifecycle stage adds the same fixed amount of rework regardless of what artifacts were affected.
    3. C. It claims late-discovered constraints always force a complete rebuild of every downstream artifact, so the exact dependency structure does not matter.
    4. D. It implies correction cost generally falls in later stages because downstream telemetry makes fixes easier than catching the issue early.

  2. True or False: Constraints in the ML lifecycle propagate only backward, from deployment limits discovered late toward earlier stages.

  3. When a data quality failure introduced during data collection propagates downstream to cause compounding failures in model training, validation, and deployment, the resulting pattern is known as a(n) ____.

3.3 Where the Hours Go: Quantifying the ML Lifecycle

The DR team lost five months to one late constraint, counting both the hours already spent and the experiments those hours could have run. Conceptual models of the ML lifecycle describe which stages exist, but planning a workflow requires knowing where its hours go, and that time rarely concentrates where research literature spends its pages.

Practitioner surveys show a gap between where research attention centers and where practitioners report spending their time, with about 79 percent of data scientists in CrowdFlower’s 2016 survey naming data collection or cleaning as the activity that consumed most of their time (CrowdFlower 2016). In the full distribution (figure 4), algorithm refinement and training-set construction together draw fewer responses than data collection alone.

The survey responses identify data work as a major time sink for these practitioners, without measuring how all engineering hours were divided. In the DR project, each image must be graded by multiple ophthalmologists before it can train anything, so collection and labeling can delay useful experiments more than training does. Improving that pipeline can then reduce iteration time before model or training optimizations become the binding concern. The workflow must identify its actual bottleneck rather than assign investment priorities from response shares alone. Even so, the imbalance explains why data engineering, developed in Data Engineering, deserves explicit planning rather than treatment as setup work.

Iteration velocity governs project progress. The feedback arrows in figure 2 (the Data fixes loop from curation back to collection, the Data needs loop from validation back to curation, and the Online Performance path from production deployment back to collection) are not rare exceptions; they are paths teams traverse repeatedly. Drifting clinic images or wrong labels send the DR team back to collection, and a candidate that exceeds the clinic device’s memory sends it back to model design. Each loop consumes calendar time and compute budget, so the length of one cycle bounds how much a team can learn. Consider two candidate DR models developed over the same six months: a large ensemble trained in the cloud, which takes a week per training run, and a lightweight edge model that trains in hours.

Over the same six months, the hourly cycle tests nearly four times as many ideas.

Napkin Math 3.1: The iteration tax
Problem: Compare what six months of development buys for a rural diabetic retinopathy screening system. The first option is a large ensemble developed on cloud hardware as a laboratory baseline (training time: 1 week, initial accuracy: 95 percent), which would itself need compression before it could fit the clinic’s memory budget. The second option is a lightweight edge model (training time: 1 hour, initial accuracy: 90 percent). How do the two compare in final accuracy and in the number of hypotheses the team can test?

Math: In six months (~26 weeks), the possible experiment count is:

  1. Large model: 26 weeks of calendar time at 1 week per experiment. Each experiment improves accuracy by an assumed constant ~0.15 percentage points.
  2. Small model: 26 weeks of calendar time at 168 h/week gives 4,368 possible experiments at 1 hour each. Machine time is not the binding constraint at that cycle length, so this scenario counts only 100 of them as effective experiments, a few per working week, the pace at which a team can design, run, and learn from experiments. Because the slow-cycle team runs one experiment per week, this cap on hypotheses, not the hardware, sets the breadth ratio below; a team that could formulate useful hypotheses faster would raise the ratio in proportion.

Result: If each iteration improves accuracy by 0.1 percentage points on average, the small model starts at 90 percent and reaches 100 percent after 100 effective iterations, before applying the 99 percent ceiling. With the 99 percent ceiling applied, the small model achieves 99 percent. The large model starts at 95 percent and reaches 98.9 percent after 26 slower iterations. Both approaches end near the ceiling, and the accuracy gap between them is an artifact of the assumed gains, not a finding. The difference that survives is breadth. Under the stated cap, the fast-cycle team tested 3.8× as many ideas, so it also learned which augmentations and preprocessing choices matter, knowledge the slow-cycle team never acquired.

Systems insight: Iteration velocity is a feature. Its value lies in search breadth rather than in a guaranteed accuracy lead. When candidate systems have comparable attainable quality, shorter cycles expand how much of the design space a team can test. Speed alone cannot guarantee that a smaller model will outperform a larger one. In the DR screening scenario, the lightweight model’s rapid iteration cycle enables the team to experiment with label-preserving input transformations, preprocessing pipelines, and architecture variations far more quickly.

This cumulative delay defines the iteration tax: time spent waiting on slow training runs or cumbersome validation pipelines reduces how many candidate models a team can test. Late discoveries amplify this penalty,7 as the constraint propagation principle (section 3.2) established. Violations caught only after deployment invalidate artifacts produced by earlier stages, forcing expensive retraining, pipeline adjustments, and renewed validation. Keeping that tax bounded requires assigning each stage clear ownership of its constraints and verifying every artifact before it crosses a stage hand-off (section 3.4.1).

7 Late correction costs: Boehm (1981) discussed in Software Engineering Economics how defects found late can cost more to fix than those caught during requirements. In ML systems, late-discovered constraints may require retraining, data-pipeline changes, and renewed validation.

Boehm, Barry W. 1981. Software Engineering Economics. Prentice-Hall.
Self-Check: Question

  1. In the 2016 CrowdFlower data scientist survey cited in the text, respondents indicated that data-related tasks dominated their time, with 60 percent selecting ____ and organizing data as their largest time sink, compared to only 4 percent for refining algorithms.

  2. A DR team’s validation pipeline takes a full week per candidate model. Using the chapter’s iteration tax, explain what this slow loop costs the team beyond calendar time, and name the workflow mechanism the chapter proposes to keep late discoveries from multiplying that cost.

3.4 Stage Ownership and Hand-offs

The rural-clinic failure paid that rework penalty in full because no stage owned the 500 MB memory constraint. The deployment limit surfaced only after data curation, model training, and evaluation decisions had hardened around an unfeasible target, and preventing such misalignments requires clear ownership across the system stack. The six stages of figure 1 divide project responsibilities along the D·A·M axes. Problem definition sets the governing requirements for all three. Data collection and preparation owns the Data axis, model development and training governs the Algorithm axis, and deployment and integration commits both to a physical Machine. Evaluation and monitoring own no single axis of their own; their role is verification, testing whether the three axes work together before release and continuously in production.

A single model choice in the DR project touches every stage. Problem definition fixed the clinic device’s 500 MB model budget, so model development starts from architectures designed for budgets of that kind. MobileNetV2, the book’s mobile lighthouse workload (Lighthouse models put the iron law into practice), is a natural candidate backbone, at about 14 MB of FP32 weights and about 600 MFLOP per inference. It reaches that operation count by replacing standard image-processing layers with a cheaper variant8 rather than by pruning a larger model. Choosing it immediately constrains the other stages. Data preparation must produce images at the capture resolution that keeps small hemorrhages visible, since that resolution rather than the backbone’s default sets the input size. Evaluation must measure latency on the clinic device as well as sensitivity. Deployment must confirm that weights, activations, and runtime together fit the budget. If on-device evaluation then misses the latency target, the fix loops back to architecture or input resolution, so the stage sequence in the diagram is traversed as a cycle rather than once.

8 Depthwise separable convolutions: The layer type MobileNetV2 uses in place of standard convolutions. It splits one expensive operation into two cheaper ones and cuts computation by roughly 8–9\(\times\) for typical kernel sizes (Sandler et al. 2018), which is what makes the roughly 600 MFLOP inference budget plausible on mobile-class hardware.

Sandler, Mark, Andrew Howard, Menglong Zhu, Andrey Zhmoginov, and Liang-Chieh Chen. 2018. “MobileNetV2: Inverted Residuals and Linear Bottlenecks.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 4510–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00474.
Checkpoint 3.2: The workflow cycle

Verify that each stage enforces contracts that bind upstream choices to physical deployment constraints.

Each of those stage decisions also lands on a term of the iron law. In the retinal screening pipeline, the capture resolution chosen during data preparation sets the bytes moved per image (\(\DataVolume\)). The MobileNetV2 backbone selected during model development fixes the operations per frame (\(\text{FLOPs/Sample}\)). The clinic hardware together with its serving runtime determines the realized execution speed (\(\text{Seconds/FLOP}\)) and dispatch overhead (\(\Latency\)). Tracing how the six stages map onto the equation shows where each team’s local choices enter the end-to-end time budget.

↩︎ The iron law of ML systems and its governing terms are established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

Systems Perspective 3.1: Lifecycle stages and the iron law
The six lifecycle stages alter distinct factors in the iron law of ML systems, both across its multiplicative D·A·M form (equation 1.2) and across its unpacked physical terms (\(\Time = \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency\)). Rather than rigid one-to-one assignments, the mappings serve as diagnostic starting points:

  • Problem definition: Sets target constraints for accuracy, latency, cost, privacy, and deployment paradigm (screened across privacy, latency, compute, and cost gates using the decision framework in Deployment Decision Screening). These targets determine which terms of the equation are permitted to grow and which must be bounded from the outset.
  • Data collection and preparation: Shapes dataset composition, the sample budget (\(\text{Samples/Task}\)), and the byte volume (\(\DataVolume\)) presented to downstream pipelines. Curation can also change the training work required to reach a quality target.
  • Model development and training: Dictates per-sample algorithmic work (\(\text{FLOPs/Sample}\)) and total operations (\(\Operations\)), along with data reuse patterns, parameter movement, and achievable hardware efficiency (\(\EfficiencyHw\)).
  • Evaluation and validation: Tests whether model quality and end-to-end execution meet deployment requirements on the target system.
  • Deployment and integration: Determines the serving path and realized machine speed (\(\text{Seconds/FLOP}\)), including runtime data movement, execution efficiency, and fixed overhead (\(\Latency\)).
  • Monitoring and maintenance: Observes distribution drift, latency, throughput, and operational cost after launch, feeding violations back into earlier stages for re-optimization.

Viewing the lifecycle through the iron law grounds latency and cost in hardware physics. Production workflows must simultaneously satisfy accuracy, safety, privacy, and organizational constraints that sit outside the equation.

3.4.1 Stage interface contracts

Assigning stage ownership across the iron law works only if each hand-off is checked before the next team builds on it. If the data team hands over \(512 \times 512\) retinal crops while the training script expects \(1024 \times 1024\) images, or if problem definition hands off to data collection without recording the clinic device’s 500 MB memory limit, the defect travels silently and surfaces only when a downstream stage crashes or rejects the finished model. In production, pipeline orchestrators prevent silent hand-off failures by encoding stage dependencies as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), where nodes represent discrete execution tasks and directed edges represent validated artifacts. While the overarching ML lifecycle forms a recurring feedback loop over weeks and months, each individual pipeline run executes as an acyclic sequence of dependent steps, allowing the orchestrator to parallelize independent branches, cache validated intermediate outputs, and halt execution the moment a boundary check fails. Just as microservice architectures rely on API specifications to govern service boundaries, ML pipelines use explicit stage interface contracts that define the required inputs, output deliverables, and quality invariants for every stage (table 2).

Enforcing explicit contracts turns the conceptual feedback loop in figure 1 into an operational audit trail. When validation or monitoring surfaces a defect, the contract boundary identifies which upstream stage owns the fix. For example, a mismatch between training features and production inputs may demand targeted data collection, whereas an error in feature transformation points directly to a bug in the serving pipeline. Catching these violations at stage boundaries stops bad assumptions before they harden into late-stage rework.

Example 3.1: Auditing stage transitions
Scenario: An engineering team claims to have completed Problem Definition for the DR screening classifier, attempting to transition to Data Collection without establishing deployment target constraints.

Diagnosis: The output contract lacks deployment paradigm selection and resource constraints (latency and memory targets). If the violation surfaces only at deployment as a trained model larger than the edge device’s 500 MB model budget, the audit must trace what relied on the missing limit. A smaller candidate may require revised input-resolution or preprocessing choices, new weights, repeated sensitivity and on-device memory tests, and a replacement deployment package. Existing raw images may remain usable; changing the candidate does not imply recollecting every image.

Repair schedule: Suppose the repair plan preserves the raw images and labels but changes input preparation and the candidate architecture. Assume 1 day to revise preparation, 2 days to train new weights, 2 days to repeat validation, and 1 day to package the approved result. If each task waits for the preceding artifact and the required staff and compute are available, the repair takes 6 days. This is a supplied schedule for this dependency chain, not a multiplier determined by the discovery stage. Queueing, failed validation, or parallel independent tests would change the elapsed time.

Systems lesson: Stage transitions act as quality gates in the ML lifecycle. Enforcing strict output contract validation before proceeding downstream prevents expensive upstream rework and ensures data collection aligns with target deployment hardware.

Without explicit contract gates, unstated assumptions propagate downstream. The earliest opportunity to bound them is Problem Definition, which records the operational constraints that subsequent stages must satisfy.

  2. An engineering team completes Problem Definition with clinical sensitivity targets but marks the target deployment paradigm as ‘TBD — to be determined after model training.’ According to the Stage Interface Specification, what should the transition audit verdict be, and why?

    1. A. Approved, because decoupling model development from hardware targets allows researchers to maximize accuracy before applying post-hoc pruning.
    2. B. Approved with warning, provided the team commits to using cloud inference if the model exceeds edge memory budgets.

  4. Which mapping between lifecycle stages and the terms in the Iron Law of ML Systems (\(T = \frac{D_{\text{vol}}}{\text{BW}} + \frac{O}{R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}} + L_{\text{lat}}\)) is conceptually consistent with the chapter?

3.5 Stage 1: Problem Definition

Problem definition in ML begins with sentences that look deceptively simple. A product manager writes: “Build a model that detects diabetic retinopathy.” That single sentence conceals a dozen engineering decisions: sensitivity thresholds for patient safety, hardware capabilities in rural clinics, latency budgets that keep clinicians engaged, and regulatory frameworks governing approval. Software requirements still govern control flow, interfaces, and data storage. ML adds an empirical obligation. Defining what the system should do is inseparable from defining how it will learn from data and what physical constraints its execution must survive.

The request for a model must first become a requirement for a useful improvement. In the clinic case, the baseline is the existing screening and referral workflow, evaluated on the outcomes and resource limits the proposed system must improve. A simpler rule or an existing model can provide an additional comparison where appropriate. The comparison must include the complexity tax established in Four screening gates for choosing a tier, so a learned system earns its place only when the value of the errors it avoids exceeds the infrastructure and maintenance it adds. If available data cannot support the required evaluation, or the expected benefit does not justify the added errors, cost, and maintenance, the team should revise the problem or retain the baseline rather than proceed automatically to training.

Balancing these demands requires reasoning across the full system stack. An engineering team cannot optimize accuracy in isolation, because pursuing marginal sensitivity gains by scaling model depth or adopting a larger model family inflates parameter count and computational demands (FLOPs per inference), directly challenging the memory capacity and thermal limits of low-cost clinic hardware. Conversely, pruning a model (removing the weights that contribute least) to fit an embedded accelerator’s DRAM budget risks dropping sensitivity below clinical safety thresholds. Every technical decision in problem definition therefore operates as a multi-constraint optimization problem in which accuracy targets and deployment limits restrict each other from day one.

3.5.1 Constraint layers

A screening model that reaches 95 percent sensitivity in a cloud notebook still fails that optimization if it needs 4 GB of RAM on a clinic device with 500 MB to spare, or if clinic staff cannot audit why it flagged a patient. To make all of those limits explicit before data collection begins, problem definition decomposes requirements into three interacting constraint layers: statistical, physical, and operational. Rather than isolated checklists, these layers form a coupled stack where a limit in one tier narrows the choices available in the others. At the top, the statistical layer specifies target behavior over data distributions, such as achieving \(>90\) percent sensitivity and \(>80\) percent specificity across diverse patient demographics and camera models rather than an unweighted aggregate accuracy that conceals failure modes on minority subpopulations. Meeting that target in the field depends on the physical layer, which bounds execution within hardware limits. For rural clinics, that means running inference on local edge accelerators under tight thermal budgets and bounded memory capacity (such as 4 to 8 GB of shared DRAM) and returning a result during the visit without depending on intermittent network uplinks. Surrounding both is the operational layer, which governs clinical auditability, regulatory clearance, and the maintenance rules that keep the deployed system safe over time.

The operational layer carries physical consequences that traditional software engineering rarely encounters. In a traditional database, deleting a patient’s record is a DELETE statement. In an ML system, model weights may encode statistical patterns learned from that record. Where law or a regulator requires removing that influence, compliance may require machine unlearning (Cao and Yang 2015; Bourtoule et al. 2021) (an active research area with incomplete guarantees) or retraining on the remaining data. The obligation and remedy depend on the governing rule and system; at DR-system scale, either can make privacy compliance a recurring compute-budget item and training provenance an architectural constraint.

Cao, Yinzhi, and Junfeng Yang. 2015. “Towards Making Systems Forget with Machine Unlearning.” 2015 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy, 463–80. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp.2015.35.
Bourtoule, Lucas, Varun Chandrasekaran, Christopher A. Choquette-Choo, Hengrui Jia, Adelin Travers, Baiwu Zhang, David Lie, and Nicolas Papernot. 2021. “Machine Unlearning.” 2021 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (SP), 141–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp40001.2021.00019.

↪ Why removing a record’s influence from trained weights remains a compliance obligation is developed in Responsible Engineering.

The constraint propagation principle (section 3.2) explains why all three layers must be bound before development begins. A constraint that exists in the physical or operational environment but remains unstated in the problem definition does not vanish. It re-emerges later as an architectural violation after dependent decisions have hardened. For example, if an engineering team defines the objective solely as aggregate classification accuracy on retinal images, standard optimization will minimize the average training loss, which the majority class dominates. In an imbalanced screening population where 90 percent of patients are healthy, a model predicting “no disease” achieves 90 percent aggregate accuracy while failing every patient who needs urgent care. Preventing this requires translating a domain invariant (a missed diagnosis costs far more than a false referral) into an explicit statistical contract: class-weighted loss, a sensitivity floor, and subgroup targets.

War Story 3.1: When the label was the bias (2018)
Context: In 2014, Amazon assembled an engineering team at its Edinburgh hub to build an internal machine-learning system for ranking technical job applicants, training it on ten years of resumes submitted to Amazon (Dastin 2018).

Mechanism: Because prior hiring was male-dominated, the model learned that male-coded language correlated with hiring success, systematically penalizing terms like “women’s” and downgrading graduates of two all-women’s colleges.

Impact: The model reproduced historical hiring bias, rendering automated resume screening unsuitable for production recruitment.

Fix: Amazon abandoned the experimental tool after attempts to remove explicit gender signals could not guarantee that it would not learn other discriminatory proxies.

Systems lesson: Problem definition must specify fairness, auditability, and rejection criteria before data collection and training. A workflow trained on biased labels does not learn the operational goal; it learns to reproduce the labels.

Dastin, Jeffrey. 2018. “Amazon Scraps Secret AI Recruiting Tool That Showed Bias Against Women.” Reuters.

3.5.2 Problem definitions evolve

When a DR screening program expands from one pilot clinic with a calibrated fundus camera to dozens of regional health centers with different camera brands and patient populations,9 its initial assumptions break. Sensor noise profiles diverge, optical resolutions shift, and demographic changes alter subgroup error rates. A problem definition is therefore not a static specification frozen at the start of a project; it must evolve as deployment expands, adding subgroup accuracy targets and broader hardware compatibility checks. How such subgroup and governance requirements are specified, tested, and maintained is developed in Responsible Engineering.

9 Demographic drift: Population changes can expose performance gaps that aggregate evaluation obscures. Facial-analysis benchmarks found large demographic error-rate disparities (Buolamwini and Gebru 2018), while a healthcare risk-scoring study found racially biased allocation from a proxy label despite similar need (Obermeyer et al. 2019). These examples come from other domains; they illustrate why a growing DR program should evaluate subgroup performance.

Buolamwini, Joy, and Timnit Gebru. 2018. “Gender Shades: Intersectional Accuracy Disparities in Commercial Gender Classification.” Conference on Fairness, Accountability and Transparency, 77–91.
Obermeyer, Ziad, Brian Powers, Christine Vogeli, and Sendhil Mullainathan. 2019. “Dissecting Racial Bias in an Algorithm Used to Manage the Health of Populations.” Science 366 (6464): 447–53. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aax2342.

This evolution follows directly from how ML systems learn. Scaling exposes rare inputs absent from pilot evaluations, while production environments shift the input distribution in ways no static offline dataset fully captures. A sound problem definition anticipates this drift by setting governance rules upfront: defining which monitoring signals trigger formal re-evaluation and bounding how threshold updates propagate through later pipeline stages. Once these operational constraints and performance boundaries are defined on paper, the workflow must ground them in physical artifacts, beginning with the data collection pipelines that gather the training distribution.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does the statement ‘Build a computer vision model that detects diabetic retinopathy’ fail as a complete problem definition for an ML system?

    1. A. It specifies only a high-level task while omitting the statistical constraint layers (sensitivity/specificity floors across subgroups), physical constraints (edge device memory/latency budgets), and operational constraints (regulatory compliance, clinical workflow integration).
    2. B. It fails to specify which exact deep neural network backbone and learning rate schedule must be used during training.
    3. C. It defines an image classification problem when medical AI systems must always be framed as unsupervised anomaly detection tasks.
    4. D. It defines quantifiable objectives before data collection has occurred, which violates standard ML agile practices.

  2. Explain why ophthalmologists and clinic administrators must participate directly in Problem Definition for a DR screening system, rather than being consulted only during clinical evaluation.

  3. In the 2018 Amazon automated recruiting war story cited in the chapter, an ML model trained on ten years of resumes was abandoned because it systematically penalized female applicants. What fundamental systems lesson does this case illustrate regarding Problem Definition?

    1. A. Resume screening models require recurrent neural networks rather than transformer architectures to avoid learning gendered proxies.
    2. B. Offline evaluation metrics are inherently incapable of measuring demographic disparities in supervised learning models.
    3. C. A model trained on historical data learns to reproduce historical label biases rather than the intended operational goal; fairness criteria and auditability must be explicitly defined at Problem Definition.
    4. D. Multi-class classification algorithms should not be applied to human evaluation tasks where ground truth is subjective.

  4. True or False: When a diabetic retinopathy screening deployment scales from a 3-clinic pilot to 200 clinics across diverse regions, the high-level clinical intent (detect referable retinopathy early) remains stable, but the specific engineering targets (subgroup sensitivity thresholds, device latency budgets, and camera-specific preprocessing rules) must evolve.

See Answers →

3.6 Stage 2: Data Collection and Preparation

On paper, the problem definition asks for 90 percent sensitivity across rural clinics; in the field, that target immediately meets smudged camera lenses, missing patient records, and a 2 Mbps clinic internet link. Moving from problem definition to data collection is where abstract accuracy targets meet those physical realities. This stage determines dataset size \((\DatasetSize)\), the number of examples, and through it the iron law’s byte volume \((\DataVolume)\), the bytes that storage systems, networks, and memory buses must move. The two grow together but are not the same quantity, since image resolution and encoding set how many bytes each example costs. The deployment constraints established during problem definition become technical data requirements. If the model must run on edge devices, the collection pipeline must produce inputs compatible with edge preprocessing; if it must achieve 90 percent sensitivity across diverse populations, the dataset must sample those subpopulations sufficiently to support model training.

Data collection and preparation is an ongoing engineering discipline rather than a one-time task. For diabetic retinopathy screening, these criteria demand a collection strategy far more stringent than standard computer vision benchmarks. The pipeline must assemble roughly \(10^5\) retinal fundus photographs across diverse patient cohorts and imaging hardware, with each image graded by multiple ophthalmologists and disagreements resolved to establish consensus ground-truth labels capable of withstanding clinical and regulatory scrutiny. The annotation protocol must capture fine pathological features (microaneurysms, hemorrhages, and hard exudates) across the entire disease severity continuum. Curation also governs computational efficiency, because not all captured data contributes equally to training, and deliberate selection (The case for smaller datasets) can eliminate redundant samples while preserving the statistical coverage required for generalizable performance.

Each retinal photograph spans several megabytes, and clinical protocols capture multiple views per patient, immediately creating a physical infrastructure bottleneck. A busy rural facility generates gigabytes of raw imaging data each day, a volume that would overwhelm an intermittent 2 Mbps uplink during operating hours. Rather than saturating limited wide-area bandwidth with raw uploads, the system can execute inference locally on the edge accelerator and transmit only a compact edge summary: a structured record containing the diagnostic classification and clinical metadata. Local inference reduces uplink traffic from tens of megabytes to kilobytes per patient.

Edge summaries shrink the clinic’s daily uplink about 2,000-fold.

Napkin Math 3.2: Bandwidth vs. compute
Problem: Quantify the uplink window occupancy for raw image uploads from a busy rural clinic capturing retinal images for diabetic retinopathy screening, and evaluate architectural alternatives to relieve link contention.

Math:

  1. Daily data: At 100 patients/day, 4 photos/patient, and 5 MB/photo, the clinic produces 2 GB/day.
  2. Upload time: The uplink is 2 Mb/s, or 0.25 MB/s. Dividing 2,000 MB by that rate gives 8,000 s, approximately 2.2 h.
  3. Constraint: If the clinic requires same-shift upload during its 8 h operating window, this transfer alone occupies the link for 27.8 percent of that window and may contend with other traffic.

Systems insight: On a good day the raw upload fits the shift, but it ties up a large share of the link, and on a day the link drops it does not happen at all. A screening result that must reach the nurse during the visit therefore cannot depend on the uplink, which is the case for local inference. Uploading detection summaries (10 KB/patient) instead of images also cuts uplink traffic by 2,000×. Overnight batching of images for training remains possible and is not compared here.

Running inference at the clinic (notebook 3.2) is the data locality invariant (definition 2.3) applied to a clinic uplink. Edge accelerators such as the NVIDIA Jetson family10 make it practical, though they introduce a hardware trade-off between using a smaller model that fits a low-power edge budget and provisioning more capable on-premise devices at higher cost. The bandwidth constraint makes this infrastructure choice a data-collection decision rather than a late implementation detail. The scenario architecture combines edge devices for local inference and preprocessing, clinic aggregation servers for data management and buffering, and cloud training infrastructure for periodic model updates. It targets a result within seconds of capture and operation that does not depend on connectivity.

10 NVIDIA Jetson: NVIDIA’s Jetson family spans a wide product range, from Jetson Orin Nano (7–15 W) through Jetson Orin NX (10–25 W) to Jetson AGX Orin (15–60 W). This scenario uses an Orin Nano-class device (4–8 GB shared LPDDR5, 7–15 W power envelope) as a concrete edge target. Its memory and power budgets constrain model complexity, while selecting a larger device changes both the feasible model and the per-clinic cost.

Privacy constraints impose a similar architectural decision. Patient privacy regulations may motivate local retention or distributed training, but they generally specify protections and permitted uses rather than one required training topology. Workflows that keep raw clinic data local while sharing only approved updates or summaries add complexity to both data collection and model training infrastructure, and they still depend on the quality of what each clinic’s camera captures.

3.6.1 Lab-to-field data gap

When Google’s DR screening system moved from curated benchmark images into Thai clinics (Beede et al. 2020), images arrived from diverse camera equipment operated by staff with varying expertise, often under suboptimal lighting and inconsistent patient positioning (section 3.1.2). That divergence between controlled development datasets and real-world inputs is the lab-to-field gap. A model trained exclusively on sharp, well-lit photographs from standardized research cameras can fail on blurry or dim captures from older clinic hardware, not because the algorithm is wrong but because the input distribution has shifted beyond the training envelope. Closing that gap across many clinics is an infrastructure problem as much as a modeling one.

3.6.2 Distributed data infrastructure

If a deployment grows from a handful of clinics to hundreds, the data infrastructure becomes a distributed system whose design follows from the constraints set during problem definition. Connectivity decides transmission. Facilities with reliable broadband can stream diagnostic summaries in near real time, whereas clinics with intermittent satellite links require store-and-forward architectures that buffer data locally and synchronize opportunistically during connectivity windows. That choice propagates through the physical stack, because store-and-forward clinics need larger local storage buffers, edge inference that runs without the network, and a way to reconcile local preliminary classifications with later central review. Storage placement matters at the central end as well, since the training set is read in full every epoch, validation sets occasionally, and the regulatory archive almost never, so each belongs on a different storage tier.

↪ The cost and throughput of each storage tier, and why ML systems combine them, are measured in Storage tiers and cost.

11 Distributed clinic data: Training across clinic sites without simply pooling all raw data addresses privacy and governance constraints, but it shifts cost into coordination. Each site may use different cameras, serve different patient populations, and follow different operating routines, so the system must track where data came from and how each site differs. The workflow cost is therefore not just storage capacity; it is the engineering work needed to compare, validate, and update models across sites whose data does not behave identically.

Data heterogeneity grows alongside byte volume. Divergent camera optics, uneven clinic illumination, and variable operator training give each clinic a distinct input distribution, so each clinic operates as a distinct data source.11 The dataset this stage hands forward must therefore carry provenance metadata that records camera model, capture settings, and operator ID for every image. That provenance trail is what isolates a site-specific accuracy regression and satisfies a regulatory audit. Heterogeneity also argues for validation at the point of capture, because a miscalibrated camera discovered weeks later during a central training run has already wasted storage, compute, and patient follow-up opportunities.

3.6.3 Quality assurance and validation

A blurry retinal image that slips past quality checks does not merely waste storage. If such images enter training with unreliable labels or appear frequently in production, they distort the statistical evidence available to the model and degrade clinical safety. Quality assurance ensures that incoming data satisfies the invariants downstream stages depend upon. In the diabetic retinopathy scenario, automated quality-scoring models deployed at the fundus camera immediately flag out-of-focus or improperly framed captures, prompting clinical staff to retake the photograph while the patient is still present rather than discovering the defect weeks later during model training.

↪ Data cascades, in which uncaught collection defects compound into downstream training and evaluation failures, are developed in Data cascades.

Validation extends beyond image quality to verify proper labeling, patient association, and privacy compliance. Local validation catches capture problems on the spot, whereas centralized validation detects distributional anomalies across the full clinic network, for instance by flagging when a particular site’s images skew toward a narrow demographic range that would bias the training set. Together with the bandwidth and privacy limits set earlier in the stage, these quality checks settle what data the training set contains and what clinic-to-clinic variations the model must handle. Even a curated, validated dataset can still starve the next stage, however, if the order in which training reads it fights the storage system that holds it.

3.6.4 Sample order and delivery rate

The DR training procedure reads the training set, on the order of \(10^5\) fundus photographs, in full on every epoch, in a fresh random order each time. Whether the accelerator stays busy depends on one comparison, the rate at which the data loader (the software that reads and batches examples for training) delivers images against the rate at which training consumes them. If delivery is slower, the accelerator idles however fast it is, and random order makes that outcome more likely because it scatters reads across storage and defeats the caching and prefetching that sequential reads enjoy. The data hand-off is therefore complete only when the workflow has measured the loader’s delivery rate against the consumption rate. Closing a gap is the work of data engineering, which treats it as the feeding problem (The feeding problem), and of training systems; the workflow’s responsibility is to run the test and route a failure to the right fix.

↪ How prefetching overlaps data delivery with computation so that the loader keeps the accelerator busy is developed in Data prefetching and overlapping.

Data collection therefore does not end when training begins. Feedback from evaluation and production keeps revising what is collected, and the delivery test keeps checking whether the loader can feed what the model consumes. Building the collection and delivery pipelines is the subject of Data Engineering, and deciding which collected examples deserve training compute is the subject of Data Selection. What this stage hands forward is a versioned dataset, its preprocessing pipeline, and a measured delivery rate, which together become the raw material that model development turns into trained models.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A rural clinic screens 100 patients per day for DR, capturing 4 retinal photos per patient (two fields per eye) at 5 MB per photo.
